Even when I get a full night’s sleep, I still feel like I have to push myself through grading, dinner, and getting ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything. I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons. But most days, underneath that, it’s like this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. Even things I usually like, like my garden or watercolors, feel sort of far away, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been about two years. Maybe a little over—like two years and a couple months. It started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. I kept thinking I’d adjust and it would pass, so I didn’t really want to make a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned things you usually like feel sort of far away; are you still able to enjoy them at all when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. It’s not like I never enjoy anything—I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. Sometimes once I’m actually out in the garden or painting, there are moments where it feels nice. But it takes more effort to start, and the enjoyment doesn’t really… last the way it used to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in and look forward to?\n\nPatient: I do still have little moments, yes. Like with my students, I can smile and get into the lesson enough, and sometimes gardening or doing watercolors can feel nice once I’m actually doing it. I guess I just don’t look forward to them the same way I used to… they feel kind of far away most of the time. It’s not that there’s nothing good, though. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel as much pleasure as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really as much, no… I mean, I can still smile with my students and get through the lessons, and sometimes it genuinely feels okay in the moment. But underneath, most days, there’s still this gray, flat feeling. Even things I used to really love, like my garden or watercolors, feel kind of far away now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, at least somewhat. I can still do things, and I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped caring. But underneath it, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling that follows me home. And then even my garden or watercolors—things I used to really enjoy—feel far away. I don’t know if that counts as losing interest, but it feels different than before.\n\nDoctor: Has this gray, flat feeling you've described been continuous over the past two years, or have you had periods where it lifted and then came back?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, I think… not every single minute, of course, but it hasn’t really lifted in a lasting way. If I’m being honest, it started around a little over two years ago—maybe two years and two months—when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine at home changed. I kept thinking I’d adjust and it would pass, so I don’t want to make it sound too dramatic, but… yes, it’s mostly been there since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything significant that I’ve noticed. My clothes seem to fit about the same. I mean, I don’t weigh myself all that often, so I might not be the best judge, but I haven’t had a big gain or loss or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Even things I usually like, like my garden or watercolors, feel sort of far away, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been about two years. Maybe a little over—like two years and a couple months. It started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. I kept thinking I’d adjust and it would pass, so I didn’t really want to make a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned things you usually like feel sort of far away; are you still able to enjoy them at all when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. It’s not like I never enjoy anything—I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. Sometimes once I’m actually out in the garden or painting, there are moments where it feels nice. But it takes more effort to start, and the enjoyment doesn’t really… last the way it used to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in and look forward to?\n\nPatient: I do still have little moments, yes. Like with my students, I can smile and get into the lesson enough, and sometimes gardening or doing watercolors can feel nice once I’m actually doing it. I guess I just don’t look forward to them the same way I used to… they feel kind of far away most of the time. It’s not that there’s nothing good, though. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel as much pleasure as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really as much, no… I mean, I can still smile with my students and get through the lessons, and sometimes it genuinely feels okay in the moment. But underneath, most days, there’s still this gray, flat feeling. Even things I used to really love, like my garden or watercolors, feel kind of far away now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, at least somewhat. I can still do things, and I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped caring. But underneath it, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling that follows me home. And then even my garden or watercolors—things I used to really enjoy—feel far away. I don’t know if that counts as losing interest, but it feels different than before.\n\nDoctor: Has this gray, flat feeling you've described been continuous over the past two years, or have you had periods where it lifted and then came back?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, I think… not every single minute, of course, but it hasn’t really lifted in a lasting way. If I’m being honest, it started around a little over two years ago—maybe two years and two months—when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine at home changed. I kept thinking I’d adjust and it would pass, so I don’t want to make it sound too dramatic, but… yes, it’s mostly been there since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything significant that I’ve noticed. My clothes seem to fit about the same. I mean, I don’t weigh myself all that often, so I might not be the best judge, but I haven’t had a big gain or loss or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things like anyone does, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big new thing lately. Mostly it’s more… just trying to keep going, I guess. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past two years where you felt completely back to normal, without this gray, flat feeling, for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a couple of months where I felt really back to normal. It’s been there in some form since about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. There are better days, definitely, but not a full stretch where it was just gone, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where this gray, flat feeling was significantly worse than it is now?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there have been big dramatic dips, exactly. It’s more that it’s just… kind of consistently there. Most days I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so I know I’m functioning. But underneath, when I get home, it feels gray and flat, like even things I usually love—my garden, or painting with watercolors—are sort of far away from me.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “significantly worse,” though. It’s more steady than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel as much pleasure as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really as much, no… I mean, I can still smile with my students and get through the lessons, and sometimes it genuinely feels okay in the moment. But underneath, most days, there’s still this gray, flat feeling. Even things I used to really love, like my garden or watercolors, feel kind of far away now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, at least somewhat. I can still do things, and I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped caring. But underneath it, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling that follows me home. And then even my garden or watercolors—things I used to really enjoy—feel far away. I don’t know if that counts as losing interest, but it feels different than before.\n\nDoctor: Has this gray, flat feeling you've described been continuous over the past two years, or have you had periods where it lifted and then came back?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, I think… not every single minute, of course, but it hasn’t really lifted in a lasting way. If I’m being honest, it started around a little over two years ago—maybe two years and two months—when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine at home changed. I kept thinking I’d adjust and it would pass, so I don’t want to make it sound too dramatic, but… yes, it’s mostly been there since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything significant that I’ve noticed. My clothes seem to fit about the same. I mean, I don’t weigh myself all that often, so I might not be the best judge, but I haven’t had a big gain or loss or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things like anyone does, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big new thing lately. Mostly it’s more… just trying to keep going, I guess. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past two years where you felt completely back to normal, without this gray, flat feeling, for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a couple of months where I felt really back to normal. It’s been there in some form since about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. There are better days, definitely, but not a full stretch where it was just gone, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where this gray, flat feeling was significantly worse than it is now?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there have been big dramatic dips, exactly. It’s more that it’s just… kind of consistently there. Most days I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so I know I’m functioning. But underneath, when I get home, it feels gray and flat, like even things I usually love—my garden, or painting with watercolors—are sort of far away from me.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “significantly worse,” though. It’s more steady than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel like you're just going through the motions without any real pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped. I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, and I still go out to the garden sometimes or get out my watercolors.\n\nBut it feels different now. Like I’m doing the motions more than really enjoying them. Underneath everything there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling, and when I come home it’s still there. Even the garden or painting can feel sort of… far away from me, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks in the past two years where you didn't feel this gray, flat feeling at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had better days here and there, but I don’t think there’s been even a full two weeks where it was completely gone. It’s been kind of present since… about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s been there in some form since about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. There are better days, definitely, but not a full stretch where it was just gone, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where this gray, flat feeling was significantly worse than it is now?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there have been big dramatic dips, exactly. It’s more that it’s just… kind of consistently there. Most days I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so I know I’m functioning. But underneath, when I get home, it feels gray and flat, like even things I usually love—my garden, or painting with watercolors—are sort of far away from me.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “significantly worse,” though. It’s more steady than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel like you're just going through the motions without any real pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped. I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, and I still go out to the garden sometimes or get out my watercolors.\n\nBut it feels different now. Like I’m doing the motions more than really enjoying them. Underneath everything there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling, and when I come home it’s still there. I mean, I’ve had better days here and there, but I don’t think there’s been even a full two weeks where it was completely gone. It’s been kind of present since… about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling has been mild, moderate, or severe most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say mild, most of the time. I mean, I’m still functioning—I can smile for my students and get through my lessons. So I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut underneath, it’s usually there… that gray, flat feeling. Yes, I think that’s a good way to put it. I can still get some pleasure from things—like being with my students, or spending time in the garden, or painting a little—but it’s just… muted, I guess.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I came in because I’ve just been really tired all the time. I know everyone’s tired, especially teaching, so maybe I’m making too much of it, but it doesn’t feel like the normal end-of-the-day teacher tired. Even when I get a full night’s sleep, I still feel like I have to push myself through grading, dinner, and getting ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything. I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons. But most days, underneath that, it’s like this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. Even things I usually like, like my garden or watercolors, feel sort of far away, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been about two years. Maybe a little over—like two years and a couple months. It started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. I kept thinking I’d adjust and it would pass, so I didn’t really want to make a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned things you usually like feel sort of far away; are you still able to enjoy them at all when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. It’s not like I never enjoy anything—I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. Sometimes once I’m actually out in the garden or painting, there are moments where it feels nice. But it takes more effort to start, and the enjoyment doesn’t really… last the way it used to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in and look forward to?\n\nPatient: I do still have little moments, yes. Like with my students, I can smile and get into the lesson enough, and sometimes gardening or doing watercolors can feel nice once I’m actually doing it. I guess I just don’t look forward to them the same way I used to… they feel kind of far away most of the time. It’s not that there’s nothing good, though. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel as much pleasure as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really as much, no… I mean, I can still smile with my students and get through the lessons, and sometimes it genuinely feels okay in the moment. But underneath, most days, there’s still this gray, flat feeling. Even things I used to really love, like my garden or watercolors, feel kind of far away now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, at least somewhat. I can still do things, and I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped caring. I mean, I still do the things, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped. I mean, I’ve had better days here and there, but I don’t think there’s been even a full two weeks where it was completely gone. It’s been kind of present since… about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling has been mild, moderate, or severe most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say mild, most of the time. I mean, I’m still functioning—I can smile for my students and get through my lessons. So I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut underneath, it’s usually there… that gray, flat feeling. It follows me home, and even things like my garden or watercolors can feel kind of far away. So… mild, I think? Does that sound right?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, but the pleasure feels less intense than it used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a good way to put it. I can still get some pleasure from things—like being with my students, or spending time in the garden, or painting a little—but it’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nMost days I can smile and get through the lessons, and I don’t think anyone would necessarily notice anything wrong. I don’t think it really goes away completely. There are times I can sort of set it aside—like when I’m with my students and I’m focused on the lesson, I can smile and seem fine—but underneath it’s still kind of there.

And then when I get home, it’s more noticeable again… that gray, flat feeling. Even my garden or watercolors can feel far away. So I wouldn’t say it disappears and comes back, exactly. It’s more like it gets quieter sometimes, if that makes sense.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I came in because I’ve just been really tired all the time. I know everyone’s tired, especially teaching, so maybe I’m making too much of it, but it doesn’t feel like the normal end-of-the-day teacher tired. Even when I get a full night’s sleep, I still feel like I have to push myself through grading, dinner, and getting ready for the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything. I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons. But most days, underneath that, it’s like this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. Even things I usually like, like my garden or watercolors, feel sort of far away, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been about two years. Maybe a little over—like two years and a couple months. It started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. I kept thinking I’d adjust and it would pass, so I didn’t really want to make a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned things you usually like feel sort of far away; are you still able to enjoy them at all when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. It’s not like I never enjoy anything—I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. Sometimes once I’m actually out in the garden or painting, there are moments where it feels nice. But it takes more effort to start, and the enjoyment doesn’t really… last the way it used to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in and look forward to?\n\nPatient: I do still have little moments, yes. Like with my students, I can smile and get into the lesson enough, and sometimes gardening or doing watercolors can feel nice once I’m actually doing it. I guess I just don’t look forward to them the same way I used to… they feel kind of far away most of the time. It’s not that there’s nothing good, though. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel as much pleasure as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really as much, no… I mean, I can still smile with my students and get through the lessons, and sometimes it genuinely feels okay in the moment. But underneath, most days, there’s still this gray, flat feeling. Even things I used to really love, like my garden or watercolors, feel kind of far away now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, at least somewhat. I can still do things, and I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped caring. But underneath it, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling that follows me home. And then even my garden or watercolors—things I used to really enjoy—feel far away. I don’t know if that counts as losing interest, but it feels different than before.\n\nDoctor: Has this gray, flat feeling you've described been continuous over the past two years, or have you had periods where it lifted and then came back?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, I think… not every single minute, of course, but it hasn’t really lifted in a lasting way. If I’m being honest, it started around a little over two years ago—maybe two years and two months—when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine at home changed. I kept thinking I’d adjust and it would pass, so I don’t want to make it sound too dramatic, but… yes, it’s mostly been there since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything significant that I’ve noticed. It’s more steady than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel like you're just going through the motions without any real pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped. I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, and I still go out to the garden sometimes or get out my watercolors.\n\nBut it feels different now. Like I’m doing the motions more than really enjoying them. Underneath everything there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling, and when I come home it’s still there. Even the garden or painting can feel sort of… far away from me, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks in the past two years where you didn't feel this gray, flat feeling at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had better days here and there, but I don’t think there’s been even a full two weeks where it was completely gone. It’s been kind of present since… about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling has been mild, moderate, or severe most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say mild, most of the time. I mean, I’m still functioning—I can smile for my students and get through my lessons. So I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut underneath, it’s usually there… that gray, flat feeling. It follows me home, and even things like my garden or watercolors can feel kind of far away. So… mild, I think? Does that sound right?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, but the pleasure feels less intense than it used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a good way to put it. I can still get some pleasure from things—like being with my students, or spending time in the garden, or painting a little—but it’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nMost days I can smile and get through the lessons, and I don’t think anyone would necessarily notice anything wrong. But underneath there’s still that gray, flat feeling, and when I get home it kind of follows me. Even the things I usually care about can feel sort of distant. I still do them, and sometimes they help a little, but it’s not the same kind of enjoyment it used to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where this gray, flat feeling went away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really goes away completely. There are times I can sort of set it aside—like when I’m with my students and I’m focused on the lesson, I can smile and seem fine—but underneath it’s still kind of there.\n\nAnd then when I get home, it’s more noticeable again… that gray, flat feeling. Even my garden or watercolors can feel far away. So I wouldn’t say it disappears and comes back, exactly. It’s more like it gets quieter sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons. But most days, underneath that, it’s like this gray, flat feeling that kind of follows me home. Even things I usually like, like my garden or watercolors, feel sort of far away, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been about two years. Maybe a little over—like two years and a couple months. It started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. I kept thinking I’d adjust and it would pass, so I didn’t really want to make a big deal of it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned things you usually like feel sort of far away; are you still able to enjoy them at all when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. I mean, I don’t weigh myself all that often, so I might not be the best judge, but I haven’t had a big gain or loss or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things like anyone does, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big new thing lately. Mostly it’s more… just trying to keep going, I guess. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past two years where you felt completely back to normal, without this gray, flat feeling, for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a couple of months where I felt really back to normal. It’s been there in some form since about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. There are better days, definitely, but not a full stretch where it was just gone, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where this gray, flat feeling was significantly worse than it is now?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there have been big dramatic dips, exactly. It’s more that it’s just… kind of consistently there. Most days I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so I know I’m functioning. But underneath, when I get home, it feels gray and flat, like even things I usually love—my garden, or painting with watercolors—are sort of far away from me.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “significantly worse,” though. It’s more steady than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel like you're just going through the motions without any real pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped. I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, and I still go out to the garden sometimes or get out my watercolors.\n\nBut it feels different now. Like I’m doing the motions more than really enjoying them. Underneath everything there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling, and when I come home it’s still there. Even the garden or painting can feel sort of… far away from me, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks in the past two years where you didn't feel this gray, flat feeling at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had better days here and there, but I don’t think there’s been even a full two weeks where it was completely gone. It’s been kind of present since… about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling has been mild, moderate, or severe most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say mild, most of the time. I mean, I’m still functioning—I can smile for my students and get through my lessons. So I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut underneath, it’s usually there… that gray, flat feeling. It follows me home, and even things like my garden or watercolors can feel kind of far away. So… mild, I think? Does that sound right?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, but the pleasure feels less intense than it used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a good way to put it. I can still get some pleasure from things—like being with my students, or spending time in the garden, or painting a little—but it’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nMost days I can smile and get through the lessons, and I don’t think anyone would necessarily notice anything wrong. But underneath there’s still that gray, flat feeling, and when I get home it kind of follows me. Even the things I usually care about can feel sort of distant. I still do them, and sometimes they help a little, but it’s not the same kind of enjoyment it used to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where this gray, flat feeling went away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really goes away completely. There are times I can sort of set it aside—like when I’m with my students and I’m focused on the lesson, I can smile and seem fine—but underneath it’s still kind of there.\n\nAnd then when I get home, it’s more noticeable again… that gray, flat feeling. Even my garden or watercolors can feel far away. So I wouldn’t say it disappears and comes back, exactly. It’s more like it gets quieter sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I can still show up and smile for my students and get through my lessons.\n\nBut most days, underneath that, it’s like this gray, flat feeling is there for a lot of the day. Does that answer it okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But it takes more effort to start, and the enjoyment doesn’t really… last the way it used to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in and look forward to?\n\nPatient: I do still have little moments, yes. Like with my students, I can smile and get into the lesson enough, and sometimes gardening or doing watercolors can feel nice once I’m actually doing it. I guess I just don’t look forward to them the same way I used to… they feel kind of far away most of the time. It’s not that there’s nothing good, though. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel as much pleasure as you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really as much, no… I mean, I can still smile with my students and get through the lessons, and sometimes it genuinely feels okay in the moment. But underneath, most days, there’s still this gray, flat feeling. Even things I used to really love, like my garden or watercolors, feel kind of far away now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, at least somewhat. I can still do things, and I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped caring. But underneath it, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling that follows me home. And then even my garden or watercolors—things I used to really enjoy—feel far away. I don’t know if that counts as losing interest, but it feels different than before.\n\nDoctor: Has this gray, flat feeling you've described been continuous over the past two years, or have you had periods where it lifted and then came back?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, I think… not every single minute, of course, but it hasn’t really lifted in a lasting way. If I’m being honest, it started around a little over two years ago—maybe two years and two months—when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine at home changed. I kept thinking I’d adjust and it would pass, so I don’t want to make it sound too dramatic, but… yes, it’s mostly been there since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything significant that I’ve noticed. My clothes seem to fit about the same. I mean, I don’t weigh myself all that often, so I might not be the best judge, but I haven’t had a big gain or loss or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. I mean, I do worry about things like anyone does, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big new thing lately. Mostly it’s more… just trying to keep going, I guess. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past two years where you felt completely back to normal, without this gray, flat feeling, for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a couple of months where I felt really back to normal. It’s been there in some form since about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. There are better days, definitely, but not a full stretch where it was just gone, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where this gray, flat feeling was significantly worse than it is now?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there have been big dramatic dips, exactly. It’s more that it’s just… kind of consistently there. Most days I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, so I know I’m functioning. But underneath, when I get home, it feels gray and flat, like even things I usually love—my garden, or painting with watercolors—are sort of far away from me.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “significantly worse,” though. It’s more steady than sudden, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now feel like you're just going through the motions without any real pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still do the things, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped. I can still smile for my students and get through the lessons, and I still go out to the garden sometimes or get out my watercolors.\n\nBut it feels different now. Like I’m doing the motions more than really enjoying them. Underneath everything there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling, and when I come home it’s still there. Even the garden or painting can feel sort of… far away from me, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks in the past two years where you didn't feel this gray, flat feeling at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had better days here and there, but I don’t think there’s been even a full two weeks where it was completely gone. It’s been kind of present since… about two years and two months ago, when my husband stopped working outside the home and our routine changed. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say this gray, flat feeling has been mild, moderate, or severe most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say mild, most of the time. I mean, I’m still functioning—I can smile for my students and get through my lessons. So I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut underneath, it’s usually there… that gray, flat feeling. It follows me home, and even things like my garden or watercolors can feel kind of far away. So… mild, I think? Does that sound right?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, but the pleasure feels less intense than it used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a good way to put it. I can still get some pleasure from things—like being with my students, or spending time in the garden, or painting a little—but it’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nMost days I can smile and get through the lessons, and I don’t think anyone would necessarily notice anything wrong. But underneath there’s still that gray, flat feeling, and when I get home it kind of follows me. Even the things I usually care about can feel sort of distant. I still do them, and sometimes they help a little, but it’s not the same kind of enjoyment it used to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where this gray, flat feeling went away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it really goes away completely. There are times I can sort of set it aside—like when I’m with my students and I’m focused on the lesson, I can smile and seem fine—but underneath it’s still kind of there.\n\nAnd then when I get home, it’s more noticeable again… that gray, flat feeling. Even my garden or watercolors can feel far away. So I wouldn’t say it disappears and comes back, exactly. It’s more like it gets quieter sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I can still show up and smile for my students and get through my lessons.\n\nBut most days, underneath that, it’s like this gray, flat feeling is there for a lot of the day. And then it follows me home, where even things like my garden or doing watercolors can feel kind of far away. So… yes, I think there have been stretches like that. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now you don't enjoy at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say there’s anything I don’t enjoy at all. It’s more that the enjoyment feels kind of dulled or harder to reach.\n\nLike, I may not feel pulled toward painting or gardening the way I used to, but if I actually do it, there can still be something nice about it. So… no, I don’t think anything has gone completely blank for me. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know if I’d call it severe, exactly… I mean, I’m still going to work, and I’m there for my students. And I’m still showing up for my family, so from the outside I probably look mostly okay.

But I have pulled back from some things. I’m not really mentoring like I used to, and I say no to social things more often. And the routines that used to make my life feel fuller—painting, gardening, that kind of creative stuff—those have become harder to keep up with.

So… it impacts me, yes. Maybe not in a way that stops everything, but enough that my life feels smaller than it used to. Is that what you mean?
